# R9653 — Alonso Sánchez to Charles V, Venice, 20 November 1522
RAH Salazar 9/26, f. 299–301. Images `img/IMG_R9653_I45450_P1.jpg`, `_P2.jpg`.
Not covered by Tomokiyo's article, which stops at R9632.

## Shape of the letter

f. 299r is a mixed letter: a **cipher paragraph of six lines at the head**, then some thirty lines
**in clear**, then a **cipher paragraph of two lines at the foot**, then the signature
"El Alonso Sanchez". The clear middle is the news of the siege and fall of Rhodes; the cipher carries
what could not be said openly.

## The clear middle (f. 299r), read from the image

> A xiij del presente fue la postrera que escreui a v[uest]ra m[agesta]d y dixe las nueuas de Rodas que
> aqui hauia dicho vn patron de vn nauio; despues [vinie]ron cartas y los d'esta republica me embiaron
> vn sumario, cuyo traslado sera con esta. Esta manyana han houido otras cartas y son de Candia de su
> general de xx del passado, y las tenia el de Rodas de x del mismo. Han me dicho me embiarian tambien
> vn otro sumario de las nueuas que tienen, si lo hizieren a tiempo que pueda yr con esta, yra; sino,
> embiarlo he a v[uest]ra m[agesta]d con otro. Lo que dizen es que hasta x de octubre hauian dado los
> turquos cinco conbates muy rezios a Rodas por diversos lugares, y baterias, y por debaxo de tierra
> por las minas y por debaxo de la agua, y siempre los hauian rebotado y muerto gran numero dellos —
> dizen xx mil. Pero vn secretario de la Señoria me ha dicho a mi que despues que el turquo esta sobre
> Rodas se le han muerto cinquenta mil hombres en las batallas y de dolencia; su armada de mar estaua
> muy mal en horden, y algunos hauisos hai que la persona del turquo se ha retirado y passado en tierra
> firme. Cartas de particulares no se dan, y de mi opinion [...] tienen ahun mejores nueuas de las que
> publican. Dizen que los de Rodas esperan socorro con desseo, y que si les llegasen mil infantes
> ternian por muy cierta la victoria. No hai duda sino que ahora hauria buena disposicion de hazer muy
> gran danyo al turquo y a su armada.

(Rhodes in fact capitulated a month later, 22 December 1522.)

## The foot cipher paragraph (f. 299r, last two lines)

Transcribed sign by sign, then resolved against Tomokiyo's key plus the numerals established here.
`‖` marks the three nulls the letters open with; `-s` is the plural sign (q with a double bar).

```
line 1  ‖ ‖ ‖  ta  [xo]y  vam  vuc-s  y  zid  rug  gub  a·u·n  [xo·a·u·y]  [tu]  vog
line 2  yeh-s  xig  tom  xig  cef  y  cif  xel  mog vuc  xig  [q·n·tu]  ...
```

Resolved:

- line 2 runs clean: **`yeh`+s `xig` `tom` `xig` `cef` y `cif` `xel` `mog`+`vuc` `xig` …**
  = "**cartas de Francia de xxvi y xxvii del passado de …**"
- line 1 gives `ta` es, `vam` embaxa(dor), `vuc`+s = "dos"/"-dos", `zid` a(h)un, `rug` la,
  `gub` señor, `vog` en, around letters still to be settled.

So the foot paragraph reports letters out of France of 26 and 27 October — the month in which
Francis I's position in Lombardy collapsed after La Bicocca and the loss of Genoa.

## New code values

`cef` = xxvi and `cif` = xxvii are **not in Tomokiyo's table**; they were predicted here by
interpolating the numeral run and then confirmed by the sense of line 2. That fixes the whole
`c`-block numeral series:

| code | cab | ceb | cib | cob | cub | cad | ced | cid | cod | cud | caf | cef | cif | cof | cuf |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| value | xv | xvi | xvii | xviii | xix | xx | xxi | xxii | xxiii | xxiv | xxv | **xxvi** | **xxvii** | xxviii | xxix |

Bold = established here; `cob cub cad cud caf` are Tomokiyo's, the rest follow from the ordering.
This resolves the uncertain "[xxviii?]" in his reading of R9598's opening as `cof`.

## Open

The head paragraph of six lines is faint (ink bleed from the facing leaf) and is not yet transcribed;
f. 300–301 not yet examined.
Decryption

 6b n-u-e-v-a-s si-n f-u-n-d-a-m-en-t-o y de la-s m-en-t-i-
    -> "Nuevas sin fundamento y de las menti-"
 7a ⊥ n Ꝁo ho zi ẗ4 lal ɡ ‖ ‖ ‖ xig ∞ 4 ɡ # nef ∝ # zub pac
 7b [?r]-a-[s] que [zi] n publica-r ‖ de g-e-r-o-ni-m-o [?Adorno] lo
    -> "-ras que […] publica[…]. De Geronimo [?Adorno] lo"
 8a ho [gad, written above] ta ho pef tac veg gub ao ɡ # Ꝁo ẗ4 ɡ # vog yeh xig çi xe
 8b que [se] es que me [tac] el señor p-r-o-[t]-n-r-o en carta de [çi] [xe]
    -> "que [se] es, que me [tac] el señor [?protonotario] en carta de […]"
 9a lud ho veg 3 a4 n geg ∞ m a4 vog Ꝁo ∂ qef ta ao 4 ⊥ n ꝛ[stain]
 9b [lud] que el d-i-a si-g-u-i-en-[t]-e [qef] es-p-e-r-a-[…]
    -> "[…] que el dia siguiente [qef] espera[…]"
10a zug g ho gad ſ m m Ꝁo xig q ∂ nef vuc feb lu ta ao 4 g ɡ n
10b alli y que se [ſ-u-m-?] de-t-e-ni-do [feb=tanto, R9635 f. 22r decipherment] por es-p-e-[y]-r-a
    -> "alli, y que se […] detenido tanto por espe[…]ra"
11a pi Lo 4 vog sob vuc ẗ4 a4 # n ẗ4 ∝ n ẗ4 g# veg veg lul pe
11b llega-[s]-e en [sob] do-n I-o-a-n M-a-n-u-el el qual p-e
    -> "Llegase en [sob] don Ioan Manuel, el qual pe-"
12a n pac ♀ ca zug vog sob ‖ ‖ ‖
12b n lo-s [ca] alli en [sob]
    -> "-n los [ca] alli en [sob]."

R9653_reading.md

# R9653: Alonso Sánchez to Charles V, Venice, 20 November 1522 (RAH Salazar 9/26 ff. 299-300), reading

Re-read 23 Oct 2026 (Claude Opus 5.5, REREAD_BRIEF batch b23); log in `R9653_reread_log.md`, line-by-line cipher and
decode in `R9653_cipher.txt`. Rests on: the clear text of f. 299r-v read from the DECODE images (`read_r9653.md`), and
the two cipher passages (f. 299r foot, 2 lines; f. 299v, 11 lines, which is what the "six-line head paragraph" of
f. 299r really is: its mirror bleed-through) read sign by sign with Tomokiyo's 1522 key (`key_codes.tsv`, `alpha.py`)
and the values settled in this pass. No contemporary decipherment is bound with the letter (f. 300r is blank; f. 300v
carries a second cipher copy of the same letter, seen only as bleed-through; R9654, ff. 301-302, has no images on
DECODE). Not in CSP Spain 2.

Conventions: plain = carried by the clear text or by the figures; `[?word]` partly carried (a sense-only code value or a
spelled word that needs a look-alike sign); `[word]` supplied; `[…]` unread; `[…xug]` an open code group. Spelling as
written, abbreviations expanded only where the clear text abbreviates.

## Reading

Sacra cesarea y catholica magestad. A xiij del presente fue la postrera que escreui a vuestra magestad y dixe las
nueuas de Rodas que aqui hauia dicho vn patron de vn nauio; despues [vinie]ron cartas y los d'esta republica me
embiaron vn sumario, cuyo traslado sera con esta. Esta manyana han houido otras cartas y son de Candia de su general
de xx del passado, y las tenia el de Rodas de x del mismo. Han me dicho me embiarian tambien vn otro sumario de las
nueuas que tienen; si lo hizieren a tiempo que pueda yr con esta, yra; sino, embiarlo he a vuestra magestad con otro.
Lo que dizen es que hasta x de octubre hauian dado los turquos cinco conbates muy rezios a Rodas por diversos lugares,
y baterias, y por debaxo de tierra por las minas y por debaxo de la agua, y siempre los hauian rebotado y muerto gran
numero dellos; dizen xx mil. Pero vn secretario de la Señoria me ha dicho a mi que despues que el turquo esta sobre
Rodas se le han muerto cinquenta mil hombres en las batallas y de dolencia; su armada de mar estaua muy mal en horden,
y algunos hauisos hai que la persona del turquo se ha retirado y passado en tierra firme. Cartas de particulares no se
dan, y de mi opinion […] tienen ahun mejores nueuas de las que publican. Dizen que los de Rodas esperan socorro con
desseo, y que si les llegasen mil infantes ternian por muy cierta la victoria. No hai duda sino que ahora hauria buena
disposicion de hazer muy gran danyo al turquo y a su armada.

[?Este] embaxador y ahun la señoria tienen cartas de Francia de xxvi y xxvii del passado de [?Sangerman]; alli estava
el rey de Francia […]. [?Toda]via [?este] embaxador de venir franceses en [?Italia], y dize que passando los ingleses a
su [?tierra] se perdieron dos naos dellos en la […xug], y los otros dieron a traves en Bretanya, y que han se ydo […nu]
alli. Nuevas sin fundamento y de las mentiras que […zi] n publica[…].

De Geronimo [?Adorno] lo que se es, que me […tac] el señor [?protonotario] en carta de […] […lud] que el dia siguiente
[…qef] espera[…] alli, y que se […] detenido tanto por espe[…]ra. Llegase en […sob] don Ioan Manuel, el qual pen[…] los
[…ca] alli en […sob].

Nuestro señor la imperial vida y estado de vuestra magestad guarde con el cumplimiento de la monarchia. De Venecia a xx
de noviembre de DXXII. De vuestra magestad cesarea y catholica humillimo siervo y vassallo que sus imperiales pies y
manos beso. Alonso Sanchez.

## Notes

- **The cipher, sentence by sentence.** Foot of f. 299r: *este embaxador y ahun la señoria tienen cartas de Francia de
  xxvi y xxvii del passado* is carried group for group (`ta`+t+e, `vam vuc`+r, `zid`, `rug gub`+i+a, t+i+e+n+`vog`,
  `yeh`+s `xig tom xig cef` y `cif xel mog vuc`); *Sangerman* is spelled across the page turn (s-a-n-g-e-r / m-a-n; the
  sixth sign is an 8-shaped sign read r). f. 299v: *dize que passando los ingleses a su tierra se perdieron dos naos
  dellos … y los otros dieron a traves en Bretanya* and *don Ioan Manuel* (do+n I-o-a-n M-a-n-u+`veg`) are spelled
  sign by sign with codes used for syllables (`mog`+n+`vuc` passando, i-n-g-l-e+`gad`+s ingleses, `xig`+t-e+`nef`+`vuc`
  detenido, `geg`+g-u-i+`vog`+t-e siguiente), as R9605 showed.
- *Toda-via* = `fef` + v-i-a; `fef` is not in Tomokiyo's table and sits between `faf` todo and `fug` tres, so *toda* is
  the slot-and-sense value (flagged). `ref` = *Italia* the same way (between `rec` Inglaterra and `rof` justa?).
  `zub` = *Adorno* (first slot of the z-block, before `zid` a(h)un): Girolamo Adorno, who joined Sánchez at Venice that
  winter (CSP Spain 2 no. 515, "Adorno and Sánchez"); sense-only, flagged.
- *señor [?protonotario]*: spelled p-r-o-t-n-r-o, read as an abbreviation; who is meant is not settled.
- `feb` = *tanto* from R9635's contemporary decipherment (f. 22r, found in this batch), which holds here (*detenido tanto
  por espe[…]ra*).
- Open: `xug`, `zi`, `tac`, `lud`, `qef`, `sob`, `ca`, `nu`+s, the run b-r-a-v-s-a after *el rey de Francia*,
  the two signs at the right edge of f. 299v l. 8 (`çi xe`), the stain at the end of l. 9, the word after *de mi
  opinion* in the clear (ink bleed).
Reading

R9653_reading.md

R9613, Venice, 18 June 1522 (RAH Salazar 9/24 ff. 203-207)

Sánchez to Charles V: his audience with the Signoria on the imperial reply to their peace paper (paper against paper; the Venetians complain the Emperor leaves the peace-and-league negotiation for a new truce, and that the Infante Ferdinand may enter the truce but not the peace); the Pregadi's debate on Cremona; French boasts and Lescun's news of 600 lances and 12,000 Gascons at Turin; the Turk not arming at sea, Rhodes holding out, Venice selling offices; the Viceroy of Naples at Mantua.

Transcription: none of the cipher. The cipher was not transcribed sign by sign; the text comes from the decipherment of the time on ff. 206-207.

Decipherment of the time

R9613_decifrado_f206.txt

# R9613: contemporary decipherment (clear copy), RAH Salazar 9/24 ff. 206r-207v (DECODE R9613, IMG_R9613_I45203_P3 right
# page = f. 206r; IMG_R9613_I45209_P2 left = f. 206v, right = f. 207r; IMG_R9613_I45209_P1 left = f. 207v), secretary's
# cursive. Docket at head of f. 206r: "Al Rey. De alonso sanchez de xviij de junio". Transcribed 23 Oct 2026 (r2i) from
# 1.15x crops. Abbreviations expanded only for q-stroke = que; v. ma. kept; <x> struck by the writer; ^x^ interlined;
# [?x] uncertain reading.
f.206r
01 Recebi la carta de v. ma. del postrero del passado y fuy con los desta
02 republica dixeles que su embaxador havia assi platicado sobre la
03 paz y dado una escritura a la qual por parte de v. ma. fue respon
04 dido por otra escritura que ternian por cierto de todo les havia su embaxador
05 enbiado treslado que les hazia saber v. ma. estava bien dispuesta beniendo
06 ellos a honestas y razonables condiciones porque assi como v. ma.
07 es emperador y cabeça de la republica christiana assi desseava mas que otro
08 establir y poner paz en ella señaladamente en Italia y unir las fuer
09 ças della contra los infieles y turcos que yo estava bien instruydo de
10 toda la negociacion y de la voluntad de v. mad. en ella si querian pla
11 ticar conmigo todos o diputar algunas personas para ello que lo hi
12 ziessen o que viessen que les parecia si consultado entre ellos querian
13 responder sin platicar otramente conmigo sino hazerme saber lo que
14 acordarian respondieronme que a su embaxador fue dicho que hiziesse
15 una escritura como le parecia que se havia de assentar la paz y que esto
16 mostrava que parecia bien lo que dezia pues le mandava hazer escritura
17 que parece que por parte de v. mad. se sale de aquella negociacion primera
18 de paz y liga que ellos querian por mas vinculo y se entrava en
19 hazer tregua nueva y que de otra parte se escrive que en la paz no puede
20 prometer por el serenissimo señor Infante y que en la tregua dize
21 que su alteza tambien entrara tambien me dixeron que el cardenal
22 de Inglaterra havia dicho a sus embaxadores queria ver las dichas
23 escrituras y dezir su parecer yo replique que si consideravan bien todo
24 lo que por parte de v. mad. se havia respondido conocerian el buen
25 animo y voluntad de v. mad. y que debian bien considerar el
26 postrero capitulo que pide v. mad. al Rey de Inglaterra por conci
27 liador que ponderasse bien aquello que v. mad. pedia lo que ellos havian
28 de pedir y verian si havia gana de tener les lo que se assentava
29 esto dixe porque se que uno de los fundamentos que hazen es que no
30 havia de cumplirseles lo que se les prometeria y a lo del señor
f.206v
31 Infante dixe que v. mad. como havia de prometer lo que no estava
32 en su mano ni era suyo y tambien les dixe que yo [?nescia] v. mad. lo
33 dezia porque ya sabian lo que fue respondido a su embaxador quando
34 propuso el volverse una parte a otra como antes de la guerra
35 que aquellas condiciones v. mad. no las havia acceptado ni nesci
36 dad que era para las acceptar replicaron que no las havia v. mad. total
37 mente dessechado a lo que me dezian del salir v. mad. de la pri
38 mera negociacion les dixe que no recibiessen engaño que no se <salia> salia
39 della antes era entrar en ella con todo fundamento y dezir y qui
40 tar las causas que lo podian destorvar que si venian a las condiciones
41 en la escritura de v. mad. contenidas verian que no era desviarse nada
42 de la primera negociacion de la paz y que si ellos pedian liga para defension
43 que v. mad. pedia mas liga que fuesse para offension con la limitacion su
44 puesta que se ponia pero en caso que por ser cosas de mucha importancia y
45 qualidad les pareciesse que havia necessidad de mas tiempo para
46 assentarlas que v. mad. dezia en este medio se podia hazer una tregua
47 que esto de la tregua les mostrava la buena voluntad de v. mad. que no lo
48 pedia sino en caso que les pareciesse no poder assi assentar la dicha paz
49 que veniendo a las condiciones en la escritura contenidas v. mad. no
50 hablava en tregua sino en la paz y porque conoci que se espinaron
51 mucho que v. mad. offrecia que el señor Infante prometeria la tregua
52 y dize que no puede prometer la paz pareciendoles a ellos assi aun
53 no me lo dixeron que aquello era de paz assi un gran postigo abier
54 to para que en qualquier tiempo se les pudiesse dañar les
55 dixe que ellos podian venir a tales y tan honestas condiciones que
56 v. mad. procuraria que el señor Infante entrasse en la paz
57 quedaron que <entre> entre ellos consultarian y me respondi
58 rian hame sido certificado que en Pregay que pocos dias
f.207r
59 ha tovieron platicaron mucho lo que devian de hazer en entregada
60 que tuviessen Cremona y que uno dellos dixo que Dios havia siempre
61 ayudado a esta republica que assi quedaria algunos dias por passar
62 antes de entregarla que verian lo que seria que agora no estava en
63 su libertad que no podian ya bien hablar en lo que havia de hazer
64 hasta que lo estuviesse mostrando que entregarla [?ne]mora era fuera
65 de la obligacion de Francia que muchos dellos tiene esta opinion yo
66 tengo necessidad que no declararan ellos esto assi sino a mas no poder
67 y teniendo assentadas sus cosas con v. mad. De Francia
68 dizen tener cartas de tres con las braburas acostumbradas dixeles
69 su embaxador que el Rey de Francia le dixo que otras vezes Francia
70 a resistido al imperio a España y a Inglaterra y que junta con
71 esta señoria no solo resistiria mas los offenderia Mossen del
72 Escut de Carmona <de carmona> fue enbiado treslado de una carta
73 que tiene de vij deste de Turin que eran alli llegadas seiscientas lanças
74 y ^doze^ mil aventureros gascones y que havian [?sus pagos] e ya buelve
75 Andrea Gritti a escrevir les confortando los con Francia a lo
76 que siento el turco no arma por mar este año y estos lo dizen y
77 han licenciado las naos que tenia detenidas los de Rodas se de
78 fiende valientemente y con daño de los turcos pero estos conti
79 nuan todavia su armar por la mar de manera que se puede
80 comprehender no arman por el turco assi mismo me ha sydo referido
81 que arman por su reputacion y que su consul de Constantinoble les
82 ha escrito que no deve dexar de armar por reputacion pero yo
83 no creo que por esto arme que no son gente que gasten su dinero en el
84 ayre tambien entiendo que quieren de aqui adelante dar todos
85 sus officios por dineros y que lo han acordado que haziendo lo sacaran
86 mucho dinero en gran manera y suele ser el postrimero esfuerço
87 que ellos hazen quando tienen necessidad y estos señales no me
f.207v
88 contentan para querer tomar concierto con v. mad. antes entiendo
89 que algunos no pero gente del govierno dizen que podria ser que no se concer
90 tassen con v. mad. y otros dizen que no se puede concertar con v. mad.
91 El visorey de Napoles me escrivio de Mantua lo que passava el rector de
92 <mantua> Verona y Prospero le escrivio que era bien resolverse con estos
93 e yo avise a v. mad. no curare de dezirlo aqui assi me escrivio
94 que solicitasse a estos le respondiessen a lo que su rector dixo e yo ge les
95 dixe quantos dias ha y replicaronme que quando respondieran a
96 v. mad. que responderian al dicho visorrey pues todo era una cosa en
97 especial que el dicho se deternia cinco dias en Mantua y que luego se
98 partio en fin este venir de franceses los tiene assi en sospecha
99 y mientra que tengan aquella esperança no pueden dexallos sino
100 a mas no poder al señor Infante he escrito lo que v. mad. por la
101 suya de xxx me tiene mandado y continuamente escrevire
102 y servire a su alteza con la mesma diligencia que v. mad. mando
103 De Venecia a xviij de junio
Decryption

R9613_reading.md

# R9613: Alonso Sánchez to Charles V, Venice, 18 June 1522 (RAH Salazar 9/24 ff. 203-207), reading

Read 23 Oct 2026 (Claude Opus 5.5, r2i). **Rests on the contemporary decipherment of the letter, a clear copy in a
secretary's cursive on ff. 206r-207v** of the same DECODE record (images `IMG_R9613_I45203_P3` right page,
`IMG_R9613_I45209_P2` both pages, `IMG_R9613_I45209_P1` left page), docketed *Al Rey. De alonso sanchez de xviij de
junio*; line transcription `R9613_decifrado_f206.txt`. The cipher is ff. 203r-205v. Checked against the figures at the
opening with Tomokiyo's key: f. 203r l. 1 *como* ‖ *quien* ‖ ‖ `hug rug yeh xig dom pud xel` ao-#-... `xel mog vuc`
g ... `xa pac`+s `xig vum` g-4-`lal` `vuo`+e ... `ho gam vam vuc`+g ... `gel rug mef` = *recebi la carta de vuestra
magestad del pos[trero] del passado y fu[y] con los desta republica; dixe[les] … que su embaxador … sobre la paz*: the
decipherment's first sentence word for word (and `hug` = *recebi*, which Tomokiyo had with a query, is confirmed). CSP
Spain 2 no. 429 (Bergenroth's English abstract, "18 June 1522, A.24 ff. 203-206") summarises the same letter.

Conventions: text carried by the decipherment; `[?word]` uncertain in the hand; struck words left out; spelling as
written; *v. ma.* / *v. mad.* expanded to *vuestra magestad*; punctuation supplied.

## Reading

Recebi la carta de vuestra magestad del postrero del passado, y fuy con los desta republica; dixeles que su embaxador
havia assi platicado sobre la paz y dado una escritura, a la qual por parte de vuestra magestad fue respondido por otra
escritura, que ternian por cierto de todo les havia su embaxador enbiado treslado; que les hazia saber vuestra
magestad estava bien dispuesta, beniendo ellos a honestas y razonables condiciones, porque assi como vuestra magestad
es emperador y cabeça de la republica christiana, assi desseava mas que otro establir y poner paz en ella,
señaladamente en Italia, y unir las fuerças della contra los infieles y turcos; que yo estava bien instruydo de toda la
negociacion y de la voluntad de vuestra magestad en ella; si querian platicar conmigo todos o diputar algunas personas
para ello, que lo hiziessen, o que viessen que les parecia; si consultado entre ellos querian responder sin platicar
otramente conmigo, sino hazerme saber lo que acordarian. Respondieronme que a su embaxador fue dicho que hiziesse una
escritura como le parecia que se havia de assentar la paz, y que esto mostrava que parecia bien lo que dezia, pues le
mandava hazer escritura; que parece que por parte de vuestra magestad se sale de aquella negociacion primera de paz y
liga que ellos querian por mas vinculo, y se entrava en hazer tregua nueva; y que de otra parte se escrive que en la
paz no puede prometer por el serenissimo señor Infante, y que en la tregua dize que su alteza tambien entrara.
Tambien me dixeron que el cardenal de Inglaterra havia dicho a sus embaxadores queria ver las dichas escrituras y
dezir su parecer. Yo repliqué que si consideravan bien todo lo que por parte de vuestra magestad se havia respondido,
conocerian el buen animo y voluntad de vuestra magestad, y que debian bien considerar el postrero capitulo, que pide
vuestra magestad al Rey de Inglaterra por conciliador; que ponderasse bien aquello, que vuestra magestad pedia lo que
ellos havian de pedir, y verian si havia gana de tenerles lo que se assentava. Esto dixe porque se que uno de los
fundamentos que hazen es que no havia de cumplirseles lo que se les prometeria.

Y a lo del señor Infante dixe que vuestra magestad como havia de prometer lo que no estava en su mano ni era suyo; y
tambien les dixe que yo [?nescia] vuestra magestad lo dezia, porque ya sabian lo que fue respondido a su embaxador
quando propuso el volverse una parte a otra como antes de la guerra: que aquellas condiciones vuestra magestad no las
havia acceptado, ni nescidad que era para las acceptar. Replicaron que no las havia vuestra magestad totalmente
dessechado. A lo que me dezian del salir vuestra magestad de la primera negociacion, les dixe que no recibiessen
engaño, que no se salia della, antes era entrar en ella con todo fundamento, y dezir y quitar las causas que lo podian
destorvar; que si venian a las condiciones en la escritura de vuestra magestad contenidas, verian que no era desviarse
nada de la primera negociacion de la paz; y que si ellos pedian liga para defension, que vuestra magestad pedia mas,
liga que fuesse para offension, con la limitacion supuesta que se ponia; pero en caso que, por ser cosas de mucha
importancia y qualidad, les pareciesse que havia necessidad de mas tiempo para assentarlas, que vuestra magestad dezia
en este medio se podia hazer una tregua; que esto de la tregua les mostrava la buena voluntad de vuestra magestad, que
no lo pedia sino en caso que les pareciesse no poder assi assentar la dicha paz; que veniendo a las condiciones en la
escritura contenidas, vuestra magestad no hablava en tregua sino en la paz. Y porque conoci que se espinaron mucho que
vuestra magestad offrecia que el señor Infante prometeria la tregua y dize que no puede prometer la paz, pareciendoles
a ellos assi, aun no me lo dixeron, que aquello era de paz assi un gran postigo abierto para que en qualquier tiempo se
les pudiesse dañar, les dixe que ellos podian venir a tales y tan honestas condiciones que vuestra magestad procuraria
que el señor Infante entrasse en la paz. Quedaron que entre ellos consultarian y me responderian.

Hame sido certificado que en Pregay, que pocos dias ha tovieron, platicaron mucho lo que devian de hazer en entregada
que tuviessen Cremona, y que uno dellos dixo que Dios havia siempre ayudado a esta republica, que assi quedaria algunos
dias por passar antes de entregarla, que verian lo que seria; que agora no estava en su libertad, que no podian ya bien
hablar en lo que havia de hazer hasta que lo estuviesse, mostrando que entregarla [?nemora] era fuera de la obligacion
de Francia; que muchos dellos tiene esta opinion. Yo tengo necessidad que no declararan ellos esto assi sino a mas no
poder, y teniendo assentadas sus cosas con vuestra magestad.

De Francia dizen tener cartas de tres, con las braburas acostumbradas. Dixeles su embaxador que el Rey de Francia le
dixo que otras vezes Francia a resistido al imperio, a España y a Inglaterra, y que junta con esta señoria no solo
resistiria, mas los offenderia. Mossen del Escut de Carmona fue enbiado treslado de una carta que tiene de vij deste de
Turin, que eran alli llegadas seiscientas lanças y doze mil aventureros gascones, y que havian [?sus pagos]; e ya buelve
Andrea Gritti a escrevirles confortandolos con Francia.

A lo que siento, el turco no arma por mar este año, y estos lo dizen, y han licenciado las naos que tenia detenidas.
Los de Rodas se defiende valientemente y con daño de los turcos. Pero estos continuan todavia su armar por la mar, de
manera que se puede comprehender no arman por el turco; assi mismo me ha sydo referido que arman por su reputacion, y
que su consul de Constantinoble les ha escrito que no deve dexar de armar por reputacion; pero yo no creo que por esto
arme, que no son gente que gasten su dinero en el ayre. Tambien entiendo que quieren de aqui adelante dar todos sus
officios por dineros, y que lo han acordado, que haziendolo sacaran mucho dinero en gran manera, y suele ser el
postrimero esfuerço que ellos hazen quando tienen necessidad; y estos señales no me contentan para querer tomar
concierto con vuestra magestad; antes entiendo que algunos no, pero gente del govierno, dizen que podria ser que no se
concertassen con vuestra magestad, y otros dizen que no se puede concertar con vuestra magestad.

El visorey de Napoles me escrivio de Mantua lo que passava; el rector de Verona y Prospero le escrivio que era bien
resolverse con estos, e yo avise a vuestra magestad; no curare de dezirlo aqui. Assi me escrivio que solicitasse a
estos le respondiessen a lo que su rector dixo, e yo ge les dixe quantos dias ha, y replicaronme que quando
respondieran a vuestra magestad, que responderian al dicho visorrey, pues todo era una cosa; en especial que el dicho
se deternia cinco dias en Mantua, y que luego se partio. En fin, este venir de franceses los tiene assi en sospecha, y
mientra que tengan aquella esperança no pueden dexallos sino a mas no poder. Al señor Infante he escrito lo que
vuestra magestad por la suya de xxx me tiene mandado, y continuamente escrevire y servire a su alteza con la mesma
diligencia que vuestra magestad mando. De Venecia a xviij de junio.

## Notes

- Uncertain in the hand: *[?nescia]* (f. 206v l. 32, a word before *v. mad.*, perhaps a slip for *necessitava*),
  *[?nemora]* (f. 207r l. 64, "entregarla nemora"; perhaps *Cremona* abbreviated), *[?sus pagos]* (l. 74). *Pregay* is
  the Venetian *Pregadi* (the Senate); *Mossen del Escut de Carmona* is written as read (Lescun, Thomas de Foix, the
  French commander at Cremona).
- The decipherment was read on DECODE's images (3528 px across the opening); the secretary's cursive is fast, so a
  word here and there may be misread; the sentences hold throughout. Only the opening was aligned with the figures.
Reading

  the Venetian *Pregadi* (the Senate); *Mossen del Escut de Carmona* is written as read (Lescun, Thomas de Foix, the
  French commander at Cremona).
- The decipherment was read on DECODE's images (3528 px across the opening); the secretary's cursive is fast, so a
  word here and there may be misread; the sentences hold throughout. Only the opening was aligned with the figures.
Translation

R9613_en.md

# R9613: Alonso Sánchez to Charles V, Venice, 18 June 1522 — English

Translation of `R9613_reading.md` (the contemporary decipherment, ff. 206r-207v). Same holes.

I received Your Majesty's letter of the last of last month and went to the lords of this Republic. I told them that
their ambassador had discussed the peace in this way and handed in a paper, to which a reply was given on Your
Majesty's behalf in another paper, of all of which they would certainly have had a copy from their ambassador; that I
informed them that Your Majesty was well disposed, provided they came to honest and reasonable conditions, because, as
Your Majesty is emperor and head of the Christian commonwealth, so you desired more than anyone to establish and set
peace in it, especially in Italy, and to unite its forces against the infidels and Turks; that I was well instructed in
the whole negotiation and in Your Majesty's will in it; if they wished to discuss it with me, all of them or by
deputing some persons for it, they should do so, or see what seemed good to them; or if, having consulted among
themselves, they wished to answer without discussing it otherwise with me, only to let me know what they would decide.
They answered that their ambassador had been told to draw up a paper on how he thought the peace should be settled, and
that this showed that what he said was approved, since he was ordered to draw up a paper; that it seems that on Your
Majesty's side one is leaving that first negotiation of peace and league, which they wanted as the stronger bond, and
entering on making a new truce; and that, on the other hand, it is written that in the peace you cannot promise for the
most serene lord Infante, while in the truce it says that His Highness would also enter. They also told me that the
Cardinal of England had told their ambassadors he wished to see the said papers and give his opinion. I replied that if
they considered well all that had been answered on Your Majesty's behalf they would recognise Your Majesty's good mind
and will, and that they should consider well the last article, in which Your Majesty asks the King of England to be
conciliator; that they should weigh well that Your Majesty was asking what they themselves ought to ask, and they would
see whether there was a wish to keep for them what was agreed. I said this because I know that one of their grounds is
that what was promised them would not be kept.

On the lord Infante's matter I said: how was Your Majesty to promise what was not in your hand nor yours? And I also
told them that I [?…] Your Majesty said it, because they already knew what was answered to their ambassador when he
proposed that each side return to the other as before the war: that Your Majesty had not accepted those conditions, nor
[?was there need] to accept them. They replied that Your Majesty had not rejected them altogether. To what they said
about Your Majesty leaving the first negotiation, I told them not to be deceived: you were not leaving it, rather this
was entering it on a sound footing, stating and removing the causes that could hinder it; that if they came to the
conditions contained in Your Majesty's paper they would see that it was no departure at all from the first negotiation
of the peace; and that if they asked for a league for defence, Your Majesty asked for more, a league for offence, with
the limitation laid down; but in case, these being matters of great weight and quality, they thought more time was
needed to settle them, Your Majesty said that a truce could be made meanwhile; that this of the truce showed them Your
Majesty's good will, since you asked for it only in case they thought the said peace could not be settled so; that,
coming to the conditions contained in the paper, Your Majesty spoke not of a truce but of the peace. And because I saw
that they were much stung that Your Majesty offered that the lord Infante would promise the truce and says he cannot
promise the peace, since it seemed to them, though they did not say so to me, that this left in the peace a great back
door open by which they could be harmed at any time, I told them they could come to such and so honest conditions that
Your Majesty would see to it that the lord Infante entered the peace. They left it that they would consult among
themselves and answer me.

I have been assured that in the Pregadi, which they held a few days ago, they discussed at length what they should do
about the surrender of Cremona, should it come, and that one of them said God had always helped this Republic, that
some days would thus pass before its surrender, and they would see what happened; that they were not now at liberty, so
they could not well speak of what had to be done until they were, showing that surrendering it [?…] was outside the
obligation to France; and many of them hold this opinion. I hold it needful that they should not declare this except
when they can do no other, and when they have their affairs settled with Your Majesty.

From France they say they have letters of three [days], with the usual boasting. Their ambassador was told by the King
of France that France has at other times resisted the Empire, Spain and England, and that joined with this Signoria it
would not only resist but attack them. Monsieur de Lescun at Cremona was sent a copy of a letter he has from Turin of
the 7th of this month, that six hundred lances and twelve thousand Gascon adventurers had arrived there, and that they
had [?their pay]; and Andrea Gritti is again writing to them, encouraging them towards France.

As far as I can tell, the Turk is not arming at sea this year, and these men say so, and they have released the ships
they had detained. Those of Rhodes defend themselves valiantly and with loss to the Turks. But these men still go on
with their arming at sea, so that one can understand they are not arming because of the Turk; I have also been told
they arm for their reputation, and that their consul at Constantinople has written to them that they must not leave off
arming, for reputation's sake; but I do not believe they arm for that, for they are not people to spend their money on
air. I also understand they mean from now on to sell all their offices for money, and have resolved on it, since by
doing so they will raise a great deal of money; and that is usually the last effort they make when they are in need. And
these signs do not please me as to their wishing to come to an agreement with Your Majesty; rather I understand that
some, not however people of the government, say that it could be they would not come to terms with Your Majesty, and
others say no agreement can be made with Your Majesty.

The Viceroy of Naples wrote to me from Mantua what was happening; the rector of Verona and Prospero [Colonna] wrote to
him that it was well to come to a resolution with these men, and I advised Your Majesty of it; I shall not trouble to
repeat it here. He also wrote asking me to press these men to answer him on what their rector said, and I told them so
some days ago, and they replied that when they answered Your Majesty they would answer the said Viceroy, since it was
all one thing; especially as the said [Viceroy] would stay five days in Mantua, and he left at once. In short, this
coming of the French keeps them in suspense, and while they have that hope they cannot leave them except when they can
do no other. I have written to the lord Infante what Your Majesty commands me by yours of the 30th, and I will
continually write to and serve His Highness with the same diligence that Your Majesty commanded. From Venice, 18 June.

R9641 (+ duplicate R9642), Venice, 27 Oct 1522 (RAH Salazar 9/26 ff. 173-188)

Sánchez to Charles V: the Signoria content with the Pope's answer on quartering imperial troops in the Gera d'Adda; the papal nuncio at Venice says the Pope could bring Venice round, but the hope of an Anglo-imperial settlement with France keeps them irresolute; postscript: Girolamo Adorno's letter and the Emperor's of 26 Sept announcing Adorno's coming with powers.

Transcription: none of the cipher. The cipher was not transcribed sign by sign; the text comes from the decipherment of the time on f. 187.

R9641_reading.md

# R9641 (with its duplicate R9642): Alonso Sánchez to Charles V, Venice, 27 October 1522 (RAH Salazar 9/26 ff. 173-174, duplicate ff. 184-186, clear copy ff. 187-188), reading

Read 23 Oct 2026 (Claude Opus 5.5, r2i). **Rests on the contemporary decipherment of the letter, a clear copy in a court
hand on ff. 187r-188r**, filed on DECODE under R9642 (images `IMG_R9642_I45397_P1`/`P2`) and docketed *Al Rey. De Alonso
Sanchez de xxvij de octubre*; transcription `R9641_decifrado_f187.txt`. The cipher stands twice: R9641 ff. 173r-174r (the
letter, ending *en Venecia a xxvij de octubre*) and R9642 ff. 185r-186r (the same text, *Somos a xxviij del dicho y es
treslado de otra enbiada por via de Genova; esta va por Roma*). Checked against the figures at the opening and the
end with Tomokiyo's key: f. 173r l. 1 `cem` … `zud vad xig` L-e-L-a … `ho pac xig vum` g-4-`lal` `vec`+n `vum`+n =
*yo … al duque de Sessa que los de esta republica dizen estan*; f. 174r l. 3-4 `xig faf vom fec xof vuc samg# zud vad
xig` L-e-L-a = *de todo esto tengo dado aviso al duque de Sessa*; the postscript *Teniendo escrito hasta aqui recebi ayer*
is written in clear on f. 174r and continues in cipher `dal yeh xig` ∞-4-ɡ-# … = *una carta de Jero[nimo] …*. New value:
`vad` = **duque** (both places; v-block). DECODE gives R9641/R9642 as "Sánchez?" and R9642 as "N/A": both are Sánchez,
and R9642 holds the decipherment. CSP Spain 2 no. 495? (Bergenroth, A.26 f. 187) is this letter.

Conventions: text carried by the decipherment; `[?word]` uncertain in the hand; struck words left out; spelling as
written, *v. mad.* expanded to *vuestra magestad*, *su sad.* to *su santidad*.

## Reading

Yo he scrito al duque de Sessa que los de esta Republica dizen estan contentos de lo que el Papa respondio a su
embaxador a la quexa que dieron de aposentar se la gente del exercito de vuestra magestad en Geradada,
maravillando me que en cosa que toque a vuestra magestad responda el Papa bien a estos, y que esta manera de respuesta
es que el Papa haze a estos daño para esta negociacion, y que si con su santidad se puede acabar seria cosa que esto
viesse prevenido para siempre: que hablando le sus enbaxadores de estos sobre cosas que toquen a vuestra magestad,
responda de manera que conozcan su santidad tiene no menos cuydado de las cosas de vuestra magestad que de las suyas
proprias, y que llegando se ellos a vuestra magestad su santidad mirara por ellos, y haziendo lo contrario que lo
miraran mal y no como les conviene. Y sepa vuestra magestad: este nuncio que aqui esta, al qual estos tienen credito
porque es muy suyo, como tengo scrito, dize que si el Papa quisiesse, que estos se reduzirian a vuestra magestad con
dezir a sus enbaxadores lo que arriba digo y que conosciessen en su santidad esta voluntad; y que las buenas respuestas
que el Papa les da y la sperança de concertar a vuestra magestad y al Rey de Inglaterra con el de Francia los haze estar
irresolutos con vuestra magestad, porque haziendo se el dicho concierto, como ellos lo tienen por cierto, piensan
entraran en el con el medio de Francia sin haver se de dar con vuestra magestad ni dar le nada. Esto no me lo ha dicho
a mi el nuncio, pero a lo dicho a persona cierta que me lo ha referido; y tanbien ha dicho que crehe estos dilataran
quanto pudieren con buenas palabras de [?conversacion] con vuestra magestad. De todo esto tengo dado haviso al duque de
Sessa.

Teniendo escrita esta, recebi ayer una carta de Jeronimo Adorno, y [?vera] con esta el treslado; juntamente con ella me
enbio una carta de vuestra magestad de xxvj del passado, por la qual me manda havisar de la venida del dicho Jeronimo
Adorno y que traehe poder para el y para mi; y yo le scrivo con este mensajero e informo del estado en que esta la
negociacion, como me lo pide; y llegado que fuere, con el ayuda de Dios, lo que en mi fuere procurare al servicio de
vuestra magestad, como hasta aqui e echo.

## Notes

- *Geradada* (l. 4, written *engeradada*): the Ghiara d'Adda (Gera d'Adda), the district on the Venetian-Milanese
  border where the imperial troops were to be quartered.
- *[?conversacion]* (l. 25) is written *conversaçõn* with a superscript, uncertain. *[?vera]* (l. 28): "y vera con esta
  el treslado", one word uncertain.
- The letter's cipher was not aligned word by word beyond the checks above; the reading follows the decipherment.
Reading

R9641_en.md

# R9641 (R9642): Alonso Sánchez to Charles V, Venice, 27 October 1522 — English

Translation of `R9641_reading.md` (the contemporary decipherment, ff. 187r-188r). Same holes.

I have written to the Duke of Sessa that the lords of this Republic say they are content with what the Pope answered
their ambassador on the complaint they made about the quartering of Your Majesty's army in the Ghiara d'Adda; I marvel
that, in a matter touching Your Majesty, the Pope should answer these men favourably, and this manner of answer that
the Pope gives them is harmful to this negotiation. If it could be obtained from His Holiness, it would be well that
this were provided for once and for all: that when their ambassadors speak to him about matters touching Your Majesty,
he should answer so that they know His Holiness has no less care for Your Majesty's affairs than for his own, and that
if they draw close to Your Majesty His Holiness will look after them, and if they do the contrary he will look on it
with disfavour, not as suits them. And Your Majesty should know: this nuncio who is here, whom these men trust because
he is very much theirs, as I have written, says that if the Pope wished, these men would come round to Your Majesty,
by his saying to their ambassadors what I say above and their seeing this will in His Holiness; and that the good
answers the Pope gives them, and the hope of bringing Your Majesty and the King of England to an agreement with the King
of France, keep them irresolute with Your Majesty, because if that agreement is made, as they hold for certain, they
think they will enter it through France without having to come to terms with Your Majesty or give you anything. The
nuncio has not said this to me himself, but he said it to a reliable person who reported it to me; and he has also said
that he believes these men will delay as long as they can with fair words of [?conversation] with Your Majesty. Of all
this I have informed the Duke of Sessa.

Having written this, I received yesterday a letter from Girolamo Adorno, and [?Your Majesty will see] the copy with this;
together with it he sent me a letter of Your Majesty's of the 26th of last month, in which you command me to be informed
of the coming of the said Girolamo Adorno and that he brings powers for himself and for me; and I am writing to him by
this messenger and informing him of the state of the negotiation, as he asks me; and when he has arrived, with God's
help, I shall do what lies in me for Your Majesty's service, as I have done until now.

R9593, Venice, 5 March 1522 (RAH Salazar 9/23 ff. 27r-27v) decoded, not read

Sánchez to Charles V: letters of Don Juan Manuel and Geronimo Adorno sent on; his audience with the Signoria on the passage of the lansquenets through Val Camonica (he had not believed the reports until those conducting them wrote and signed them; Andrea Gritti's officer and the damage claimed); the French have taken Caravaggio, the Swiss at Monza said to be 16,000 and 4,000.

A mechanical decode with holes in most sentences: shown so that the transcription and the key can be checked and improved, not as a reading.

# R9593 = Alonso Sánchez -> Charles V, Venice, 5 March 1522 (RAH Salazar 9/23 ff. 27r-27v; DECODE R9593, 2 images).
# Date: the clear subscription f. 27v l. 19 "en venecia a v de março de dxxij" (DECODE's 5 March confirmed).
# Transcribed sign by sign 23 Oct 2026 (r2o, Claude Opus 5.5) from IMG_R9593_I45129_P1.jpg (f. 27r, right page, 33 lines)
# and _P2.jpg (f. 27v, left page, 18 lines + clear date), line and block crops at 1.75-3.5x (scratchpad reread/r2o/R9593).
# Not calendared in CSP Spain 2 (no Salazar A. 23 f. 27 entry; checked on british-history.ac.uk pp386-412, r2o).

# R9593: Alonso Sánchez to Charles V, Venice, 5 March 1522 (RAH Salazar 9/23 ff. 27r-27v), partial reading

Read 23 Oct 2026 (Claude Opus 5.5, r2o) **from the cipher alone, sign by sign with Tomokiyo's 2025 key** (`key_codes.tsv`)
on DECODE's two images (f. 27r right page of P1, f. 27v left page of P2). No decipherment is bound or imaged with the
record; the letter is not calendared in CSP Spain 2 (no Salazar A. 23 f. 27 entry); Tomokiyo printed its first line only
(*Esta noche … despache un aposta para vuestra magestad con …*), which this reading agrees with. Date from the clear
subscription, f. 27v: *en venecia a v de março de dxxij*. Line-by-line transcription and decode: `R9593_signs.txt`.

Conventions: words carried by the figures are plain; `[?word]` partly carried (sense, a look-alike, or a code value given
by its alphabetical slot only); `[word]` supplied; `[..]` unread; `[xyz]` a code group with no value; `‖` the nulls that
open a paragraph. Spelling as the figures give it.

## Reading

Esta noche passada de[?spache] un aposta para vuestra magestad con cartas que don Ioan me enbio del primero desta, con
[xe] propio, y dize me las enbie con [no] [vub]; y assi se hizo. Con la [lud] sera [fug] la [..] de una carta de Geronimo
Adorno.

Esta mañana fuy con los desta republica y dixeles que bien sabian lo que postreramente les havia dicho a[?cerqua] el
passe de los [?lansquenetes]; que yo no havia creydo lo que me hera estado escrito, pero que ahora començava a de creer
lo, por que me lo bolbian a escrevir y a firmar los que tienen cargo [la] vuestra magestad de conduzir a los dichos
[?lansquenetes]; y me dezian [..] havisados que [da] con [..] de esta señoria havia ydo a [lif]r lo y obviar el passe de
Valcamonica; que yo me quexava y dolia [ni] que [fu] hayan mendado, que era muy [sag]a de lo que de personas tan
sabias devian; que se acordasen lo que tantas vezes me han dicho.

Y postreramente cinco o [?seis] dias havia: dixeronme que quien me lo escrevia estava mal [?informado], que es verdad
que Andrea Griti havia enbiado un oficial a ver si havian echo algun danyo [la] donde havian passado los
[?lansquenetes], y que [..] que no havian echo danyo [nif]; y que assi seria partido [?aquel] oficial con la gente;
que [ram] y que esto era [..]; que [tag] y estado no acostunbra dezir; si no lo que es, dix[e]ra que si podia escrevir
a vuestra magestad en su nonbre [..] que los [?lansquenetes] podian passar seguramente. Dixeronme como yo [..]; y que si
dixera que [paf] [..] en [zom] [..]. [..] su [..] de [..] que [..] buen [fag] [pil] y [lif] [..] de [dug] y todo lo
[?necessario] por sus [..] a los [?lansquenetes] de vuestra magestad. Dixeronme que lo havia[n] echo, y que en lo que
con su honra podian se servira vuestra magestad, que lo hazian y harian. No [nad] do me [..] la dificultad que [..] en
el dicho passe [..] dezir [..] que la tiene de la passada de los otros dos [..] buen testimonio de lo que dize; pero de
donde yo supe que havian enbiado, todavia me [..]; es cierto, y que estos mienten.

‖ [..] con este mesmo mensagero dos avisos a Geronimo Adorno, y le digo que no [..] todas estas [mug]s de [..] sobre si, y
apercibidos de lo [?necessario].

‖ [..] Pregunte tambien, y dixeronme tenian cartas de Andrea Griti de [..] de dos deste, a las xvii oras, que havian
franceses tomado Caravagio y la [tem] que los [mug]s la de [..] partido; que [..] con los franceses havian passado [..]
se [..] con suyos, los quales [..] en Monza. Pregunteles de la [?quantidad] de los suyos; dieronme que eran xvi mil
[?soldados] y quatro mil [?aventureros].

‖ Uno me ha dicho esta mañana que Andrea Griti enbia a un capitan de [..] para saber el [nog] de los dichos suyos y el
[..] del dicho capitan, que es [do] no y esta aqui; ha dicho que no son mas de [ca] [ce] mil los dichos suyos.

En Venecia a v de março de DXXII.

## Notes

- **Look-alikes.** Letters resting on the other member of a pair, counted on the reading: Lo/ϙ/q read s or t or l by
  sense in *aposta*, *escrito*, *testimonio*, *Griti* (×2), *postreramente*, *aventureros*, *pregunteles* (about 14);
  ꝑ read m (*Valcamonica*, *Geronimo*, *mensagero*, *Monza*) against ǂ read o (*ahora*, *cinco o*, *testimonio*) (about
  9); ꝫ read a, but s in *acostunbra* and c in *apercibidos* (3): about 26 of some 1,050 letters, 2.5%.
- **Code values used beyond the published key** (all logged in `R9593_reread_log.md`): `gim` = *suyos* (adopted: R9635's
  decipherment, and here four times); `fef` = *toda* (adopted: *todavia* here and in R9653, *todas estas* here); `ril`
  = *lansquenetes* (five times here, alphabetical slot between `rug` la and `pac` lo; sense only, flagged).
- **Open groups:** `xe`, `no` (as a word), `vub`, `lud`, `fug`, `la` (twice: *cargo [la] vuestra magestad*, *[la] donde*),
  `lif`, `ni`, `fu`, `sag`, `nif`, `ram`, `tag`, `paf` (as menda-, *mandado*?), `zom`, `fag`, `pil`, `dug`, `nad`, `mug`
  (*palabla?* does not fit), `tem`, `nog`, `do` (not *venecia* here), `ca`, `ce`.
- The second half of f. 27r (ll. 26-33) and f. 27v ll. 11-13 do not build sentences: the run of open groups and
  uncertain signs is too dense.

# R9593: Alonso Sánchez to Charles V, Venice, 5 March 1522: English translation of the partial reading

Translation of `R9593_reading.md` (read from the cipher with Tomokiyo's key; no contemporary decipherment). Holes as in
the Spanish: `[..]` unread, `[?word]` uncertain, `[xyz]` a code group with no value.

## Translation

Last night I dispatched a courier to Your Majesty with letters that Don Juan [Manuel] sent me of the first of this
month, by [xe] his own messenger, and he tells me to send them on with [no] [vub]; and so it was done. With the [lud]
will be [fug] the [..] of a letter from Girolamo Adorno.

This morning I went to the lords of this republic and told them they well knew what I had last said to them about the
passage of the [?lansquenets]; that I had not believed what had been written to me, but that now I was beginning to
believe it, because those who have charge [la] Your Majesty of conducting the said [?lansquenets] wrote and signed it to
me again; and they told me [..] warned that [da] with [..] of this Signory had gone to [lif] it and block the pass of Val
Camonica; that I complained and grieved [ni] that [fu] they should have ordered it, that it was very [sag] from what was
owed by persons so wise; that they should remember what they have told me so many times.

And lastly, five or [?six] days ago: they told me that whoever wrote it to me was ill [?informed]; that it is true that
Andrea Gritti had sent an officer to see whether they had done any damage [la] where the [?lansquenets] had passed, and
that [..] that they had done no damage [nif]; and that thus [?that] officer would have left with the troops; that [ram]
and that this was [..]; that [tag] and the state is not accustomed to say [it]; if it were not so, it would have said
that it could write to Your Majesty in its name [..] that the [?lansquenets] could pass safely. They told me how I [..];
and that if it said that [paf] [..] in [zom] [..]. [..] its [..] of [..] that [..] good [fag] [pil] and [lif] [..] of
[dug] and all that is [?necessary] for its [..] to Your Majesty's [?lansquenets]. They told me they had done so, and
that in whatever they could with their honour Your Majesty would be served, and that they did and would do it. No [nad]
[..] me [..] the difficulty that [..] in the said pass [..] say [..] that it has from the passage of the other two [..]
good testimony of what it says; but from where I learned that they had sent, still [..] me; it is certain, and these
people are lying.

[..] by this same messenger two warnings to Girolamo Adorno, and I tell him not [..] all these [mug] of [..] on their
guard, and prepared with what is [?necessary].

[..] I asked also, and they told me they had letters from Andrea Gritti of [..] of the second of this month, at the
17th hour, that the French had taken Caravaggio and the [tem] that the [mug] the [..] departed; that [..] with the French
had passed [..] [..] with their men, who [..] in Monza. I asked them about the [?number] of their men; they told me they
were 16,000 [?soldiers] and 4,000 [?adventurers].

Someone told me this morning that Andrea Gritti is sending a captain of [..] to learn the [nog] of their said men and
the [..] of the said captain, who is [do] not and is here; he has said that their said men are no more than [ca] [ce]
thousand.

At Venice, 5 March 1522.

R9607, Venice, 14 May 1522 (RAH Salazar 9/24 ff. 65r-67v) read in part

Sánchez to Charles V: Venice's armament after the French defeat in Lombardy (galleys at Venice and in Candia, French soldiers come from Milan to Padua); Cremona, Lautrec and Prospero Colonna; his demand that the Signoria declare itself released from its capitulation with France; news of the Turk's fleet from Corfu (letters of 5 April) and of the King of France coming in person; the Hungarian ambassador's departure; the Signoria's minute of its answer. Uncalendared; opening line printed by Tomokiyo (2025).

Builds coherent sentences, but under the read bar of 95% of words read as sense (72% here); the holes are marked in the reading.

# R9607 — Alonso Sánchez to Charles V, Venice, 14 May 1522: English rendering of the read parts

Brice Dudout (Claude Opus 5.5), 12 Nov 2026, from `R9607_reading.md` (read in part, 79.5 % of 1,555 words). […] marks
words not read; *italics* mark probable readings. Paragraphs follow the null signs of the cipher.

**f. 65r.** To Your Majesty's letter of 29 April, which I received on […], there is nothing to answer, except that in the
[…] principal it is now some days since I have spoken, still less now that those of this Republic would speak, […]
since they have *seen* the […] they were waiting for and were in need, and […] very […] for fear and *suspicion*. […]
their ambassador's *petition* to Your Majesty […] which Your Majesty has not *wished* […] bad for them; and the cause,
I understand, […] the Infante […] here, and they sent to send it to their *lands* […] They told me they were keeping
[…] not to have them when they need them. They are still arming galleys, and *so far* they have armed here […] besides
those they are arming in *Candia* […]; the sea, after the French were *lost*, […] Here in this […] French and others
who were in the King of France's camp have come from Milan, […] and some of them have […] in *Padua* and other places
near here, with their […] and arms. So far no one has come from […]. Three days ago […] the people of this Republic,
*feeling* that letters had come from Your Majesty's camp, and they had told me that they had put their own troops […]
for the […] of *Cremona* […]; and that *Lautrec* with his troops […]; all this they felt because I heard it here; not
yet from persons […].

**f. 65v.** They told me that in *Cremona* there was […] a captain of theirs with […] men-at-arms, called […], whom they

R9610, Venice, 2 June 1522 (RAH Salazar 9/24 ff. 132r-134r) read in part

Sánchez to Charles V: 24,000 ducats sent by the Viceroy of Naples for Prospero Colonna to pass through Venetian lands (the Signoria refuses a safe-conduct but has the rectors of Verona escort it); a safe-conduct for the Viceroy and his company; letters of the King of France from Lyon (he will not leave the enterprise, blames Lautrec, sends the Admiral); Lautrec, Cremona and Andrea Gritti; Lautrec leaves for France; the Turk's ambassador as a spy, the Turk arming a hundred sails, Venice's fifty galleys; a procurator of San Marco made for money; the Turks before Knin. Uncalendared; opening line printed by Tomokiyo (2025).

Builds coherent sentences, but under the read bar of 95% of words read as sense (61% here); the holes are marked in the reading.

# R9610 — Alonso Sánchez to Charles V, Venice, 2 June 1522 (RAH Salazar 9/24 ff. 132r-134r; DECODE R9610)

Reading by Brice Dudout (Claude Opus 5.5), 12 Nov 2026. Source: DECODE R9610
(<https://de-crypt.org/decrypt-web/RecordsView/9610>), images P1-P3; no image is reproduced here.
Date in clear at the foot of f. 134r: *en venecia a ij de junio de dxxij*. Not calendared in CSP Spain 2 (Daniel's
entry-by-entry check, NOTES); no decipherment imaged on the record. Tomokiyo (2025) printed the opening line, "A-y-e-r en
la t-a-r-de h-o-v-e carta-s de t-r-e-n-t-o de b-a-n-i-si-o con un-a p-o-s-t-a que me"; the reading below agrees with it.

**Method.** `R9610_signs.txt` (130 cipher lines, 2,722 tokens without nulls and clear words) was transcribed blind, by
sign shape, and frozen by SHA-256 (`protocol/SHA256SUMS`) before any decoding, together with `protocol/PREREG_lot2.md`
(the key stays unchanged, so no new measure). It was decoded with `../key_codes.tsv` unchanged and the letter signs of
`../alpha.py` (`scripts/decode_lot2.py`, raw output `R9610_decode.txt`) and read by sense.

**Markup.** plain = sure; {w} = probable (a declared look-alike, PREREG_lot2 §1: g = r|y, q/Yo = s|t, P = f|s, 3 = d|e,
Z = a|s|c, B = m|r, tt = p|n, resolved by sense); {w†} = probable, but one sign of *my blind transcription* departs from
the form the sense needs, and the needed form is a sign of Daniel's table (see "Transcription errors" below); [w] =
restored; <w> = unresolved (raw decode kept); «…» = clear in the letter, not counted. The † class was not declared in
the PREREG, so the count is given both ways.

| page | sure | probable | probable † | restored | unresolved | words | read (S+P) | with † |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| f. 132r | 140 | 44 | 28 | 0 | 32 | 244 | 75.4 % | 86.9 % |
| f. 132v | 166 | 50 | 12 | 1 | 28 | 257 | 84.0 % | 88.7 % |
| f. 133r | 155 | 58 | 23 | 0 | 27 | 263 | 81.0 % | 89.7 % |
| f. 133v | 163 | 39 | 22 | 1 | 37 | 262 | 77.1 % | 85.5 % |
| f. 134r | 67 | 20 | 11 | 0 | 13 | 111 | 78.4 % | 88.3 % |
| **letter** | **691** | **211** | **96** | **2** | **137** | **1,137** | **79.3 %** | **87.8 %** |

**Read in part** under the 95 % rule either way (pre-registered count 79.3 %; 87.8 % if the † words are admitted).
Counted by `scripts/count_reading.py`.

**Transcription errors (†).** The decode shows where my shape labels split or merged Daniel's signs, always the same way:
my `q` (and some `g`) is the l-sign `9` (pol q = *mil*, Q n q m # = *salvo*, pol g = *mill*); my `oo` covers both of
alpha.py's ∞ signs, `oo` = d and `OO` = g (*seguros*, *cargo*, *pagado*, *tengan*, *Griti*); `Z` is sometimes ∂ = d
(*conduto*, *perdida*, *ronpida*, *padre*); my `P` is in places the m-sign (*almirante*, *San Marco*, *conforman*,
*Carmona*); `Lo` stands for the s-like sign (*pasasen*, *fuessen*, *pusiessen*); the pair `tt #` is one sign, alpha.py's
`jt` = u/v (*nueva*, *Savoya*, *estavan*, *Verona*, *entrava*). No value outside Daniel's table is used.

**Code groups absent from the key** (39 groups; listed in `R9610_decode.txt`): proposals only, none applied —
`sod` = *govierno* (3 contexts, s-block bracket after *governador*), `dud` = *vino* (2 here + 2 in R9607, after *vi*),
`lud` = *presente* (here "estando escriviendo la <lud>", R9607 "a [..] del <lud> recebi"), `cid` = *xxii* (c-block
numerals, "cartas de <cid> de abril"). Details in `R9607_recheck.md`.

**Names.** Banisio = Jacopo Bannisio (imperial secretary at Trento); the Visorey de Napoles = Charles de Lannoy; Prospero =
Prospero Colonna; Theodoro [Trivulcio] (spelled run unresolved); Andrea Griti; the almirante de Francia = Bonnivet; the
bastardo de Savoya; *Carmona* is Sánchez's spelling of Cremona (as in R9607); *Tnina* = Knin, besieged by the Turks.

## Reading, line by line

R9610_en.md

# R9610, Venice, 2 June 1522 — English summary of the reading (Brice Dudout, 12 Nov 2026)

A summary, not a translation, of `R9610_text_es.md` (read in part, 79.3 %; unresolved words left out). Brackets mark
what the unresolved words leave uncertain.

**f. 132r.** Yesterday afternoon Sánchez had letters from Banisio at Trento by a post: the Viceroy of Naples had sent
there a man with 24,000 ducats that Prospero [Colonna] had asked for, to be taken to the camp; once in Mantua [the
Marquis] would forward it, but to pass it through the Signoria's lands Banisio asked Sánchez to obtain a safe-conduct.
The Signoria answered that they could not give a safe-conduct, but would write to the rectors of Verona to send [an
escort] so that the money could pass safely through their lands, and asked that those carrying it warn the rectors of
Verona when they would be in the Signoria's lands, so that men would be there to accompany them. He writes so to
Banisio by this post. He thinks they decline the safe-conduct so that the French should not learn of it.

**f. 132v.** He has also obtained a full safe-conduct for the Viceroy of Naples and all his company, and sends it to
Banisio to keep until the Viceroy arrives, which will not be long: the Viceroy wrote on the 26th of last month that he
would leave [...] by easy stages, having no need to stop to raise infantry. This morning he was told they would let him
know who is to be sent to the Viceroy; before this letter is closed His Majesty will be told who it is.
A French gentleman of Theodoro [Trivulzio], whom the Venetians sent to the King of France after the loss [at the
Bicocca?], left Lyon on the 17th of last month with letters of the King of France for the Republic and for [Andrea
Gritti]: in substance, that they should hold for certain he would lose France and his life rather than abandon this
enterprise; excuses for what was done on his side; the bad government of Lautrec was the cause of all the damage; he
was sending the Admiral of France to the enterprise.

**f. 133r.** The men he is bound to [provide] would not be lacking, and they would do more; [...] the Admiral, who will
bring [...] and do what they advise. Sánchez knows for certain that Lautrec was much [...] with the Venetians and wanted
to put himself in their lands with the troops that went to Cremona; they did not allow it; Andrea Gritti even protested
that [his own men] should go to guard Cremona; Lautrec asked them to put their own troops in Cremona and they answered
they would guard their lands. Lautrec left on the 25th, they say, [from ...] on the way back to France; those of
Cremona went with him for he had [no ...]; [a hundred ...] went by the road the Bastard of Savoy took. Others say the
Admiral of France will come with 1,200 lances, [...] thousand Gascons and [...] thousand of his own; but from what is
heard, on the whole road to Lyon there is not a man of war.
The Turk's ambassador, here since the 25th, is thought to have come more as a spy than for anything else. He says the
Turk complains that [...] are made in the Venetians' lands against his subjects, and that Christians who flee take
refuge there; that they should provide otherwise, or hold the peace broken.

**f. 133v.** He also asks for [...] a Venetian, not now here, who formerly left with jewels of the Turk's treasure worth
up to [...] thousand ducats. The Venetians say the ambassador has come for nothing else, and have written so to their
ambassadors everywhere. Sánchez has had a [spy] at the door of the ambassador's house to see whether anyone of those
who keep the secrets of the Signoria goes in; so far no one has, public or [secret]. The ambassador says the Turk will
not arm more than [...] sails; the reports agree that he is arming a hundred sails; the Signoria has letters of [22?]
April saying that he was arming without it being known where. The Doge told Sánchez he does not believe [the Turk will
do] anything this year. Venice is arming with reasonable speed, but he thinks they will not complete the fifty galleys
[...] until they have surer news of the Turk's armament; then their captain will leave, and on his departure the
reports of the Turk's fleet will be known. Those of the [government] say the Turk will go against [Apulia?].
A procurator of San Marco has been made here for [...] thousand ducats paid, a new thing, for procurators used not to be
made except [...].

**f. 134r.** [...] went to take leave of the Signoria [...] has left for Ferrara. He understands that the French relief
will not come in time and that Cremona will surrender to His Majesty's men. While he was writing this, the Signoria
gave him a copy, which goes with this letter, of the news they have had that the Turks were before Knin; they told him
they have written to Verona to give the Viceroy of Naples every honour and to receive him very well.
Venice, 2 June 1522.

R9614, Venice, 18 June 1522 (RAH Salazar 9/24 ff. 208r-210v), a second copy of R9613 read in part

A copy (treslado, sent by Genoa to Geronimo Adorno) of the letter of 18 June sent by post to England, CSP Spain 2 no. 429, which this target already holds as R9613 from its contemporary decipherment; read here again from the cipher with the key unchanged.

Builds coherent sentences, but under the read bar of 95% of words read as sense (62% here); the holes are marked in the reading.

R9614_en.md

# R9614, Venice, 18 June 1522 — English summary of the reading (Brice Dudout, 12 Nov 2026, C04)

A summary, not a translation, of `R9614_text_es.txt` (read in part: 77.3 % strict, 87.7 % with the † words; unresolved
words left out). Brackets mark what the unresolved words leave uncertain. Key unchanged (PREREG_lot3 measure FAIL).
Clear in the letter: the date "de venecia a xviij de junio de dxxij" and a postscript: this is a copy of another letter
sent "con las postas" to England, now sent by way of Genoa, addressed to Geronimo Adorno. P4 left is a separate letter in
clear, in another hand (Viceroy of Naples, Mantua, Verona, Prospero), not read here.

**f. 208r.** Sánchez has His Majesty's letters of the end of last month. He went to the Signoria and told them that their
ambassador [at court] had [treated] of peace and handed in a writing, answered on His Majesty's side by another writing;
their ambassador will have sent them a copy, telling them His Majesty was well disposed, if they came to honest and
reasonable conditions, since His Majesty, as head of the Christian commonwealth, wishes more than anyone to make peace
and unite forces against the infidels and Turks. He asked them to make known what they meant to answer. They replied
that their ambassador had been told to make a writing; it seemed to them that [His Majesty] departs from the first
[proposal] of peace and turns to a new truce, while they excuse themselves that in a peace

**f. 208v.** the Infante [Ferdinand] cannot [be included]; in the truce, his [agent] would also enter. The [king] of
England had told his ambassadors he wanted to see the writings and give his opinion. Sánchez answered that if they
weighed what His Majesty had replied they would see his good will; they should consider His Majesty's last [offer] and
ask the [Pope?] (`hod`) to be guarantor. On the Infante: His Majesty could not [bind] what was not in his hands. They
answered that His Majesty had not accepted those conditions and had not wholly [rejected] them; Sánchez told them not
to take amiss what was not known, and to set out the causes that could prevent it.

**f. 209r.** If they came to the conditions contained in His Majesty's writing, they would see he would not depart from
the first [proposal] of peace; if they asked [more], His Majesty asked more [too], as a fair satisfaction. If, being
matters of [importance], they needed more time, a truce could be made meanwhile, which shows His Majesty's good will;
but he asks it only as a step to peace. They say the Infante [rejects] the truce and that peace cannot be [made]; Sánchez
said they could come to such honest conditions that His Majesty would have the Infante enter the peace.

**f. 209v.** They will [discuss] the matter among themselves. He has been told that a few days ago in the [Pregadi?]
they were [divided] over what to do with [the men] at Cremona; one said God had [helped] the Republic. They will not
declare themselves free of the obligation to France except under necessity. The French claim letters with the usual
bravado; the French ambassador says the King of France told him France has [held out] against the [Emperor] and
England. From Turin, letters of the 7th: [lances] and [x] thousand Gascon adventurers have arrived. As to the Turk, he
is not arming by sea this year; the [ships] they held are released.

**f. 210r.** The [defenders] resist bravely, with damage to the Turks, who keep arming at sea; Venice arms [for
reputation], on its consul's letters from [Constantinople], but they will not spend their money in the air. They make
money by selling offices. Some say they cannot come to terms with His Majesty, others that they may. The Viceroy of
Naples wrote from Mantua of his dealings with the rector of Verona and with Prospero [Colonna]; the rector will have
reported.

leave [Lombardy] unless forced. He has written to the Infante as ordered.

# R9640, Venice, 22 Oct 1522 — English summary of the reading (Brice Dudout, 12 Nov 2026)

A summary of `R9640_text_es.txt`, not a translation. The letter is read in part: 71.5 % strict, 80.1 % with the † words.
Unresolved words are left out, and brackets mark what they leave uncertain. Daniel's key is unchanged: the PREREG_lot3
measure failed, so no code value was added.

**f. 139r.** The [galleys] of the Venetians are detained in England, and this has made them very [uneasy]. Even those
who [govern] say openly that, because of it, they are in no state to act for France. The French party in Venice says
it cannot reach an understanding with His Majesty, because it has no one to trust. [The Pope?] (`hod`) was to have
been the mediator and the guarantor of the agreement between the two sides, but how could they trust him when those of
the French party draw everything to their own ends? The members of the [Council] are very [cautious] and dare not speak
out. Sánchez's view is that the [galleys] held in England, together with the other pressures, will bring the Venetians
round more than their own will would. The French ambassador recently went to the Signoria to complain that they were
speaking ill of his king. He also protested that his king was so wise and so [powerful] that ...

**f. 139v.** The Venetians answered that they could not [stop] what people said. The ambassador went on: Christian
princes were turning against the French, and the French would bring in the Turk to ruin Christendom. If anyone said
such a thing, [the king] would have him hanged. Sánchez recalls what he had written about the quartering of His
Majesty's army in [...]; Don Juan [Manuel] wrote him the same. The Venetians say they had a good answer from [`mif`]
about it. Sánchez writes to the Duke of [Sessa] on matters touching His Majesty. Geronimo Adorno sent a man [`sub`]
here, and Geronimo Centurion came with a letter of credence for Sánchez.

**f. 140r.** The French hold places in Picardy. It would do less harm to the French if the places of that frontier
were strong and provided. [Geronimo Adorno's plan]: with [about ... fifty] thousand ducats, xv thousand infantry and
other troops, Prospero [Colonna] and the Marquis of Pescara could carry the enterprise against the King of France
easily, with some fleet, since the places there are neither strong nor provided. That way the army could be kept up,
and [one of the King's treasurers?] would be needed to raise the money. Geronimo [Adorno] had written all this to His
Majesty and was waiting for the answer. He had sent [Centurion] here to [discuss] it with the English ambassador.
Sánchez answered Geronimo Centurion that Adorno seemed to him very wise and very good.

**f. 140v.** They were to wait for His Majesty's answer before speaking to the ambassador, and Geronimo Adorno had
[agreed] this with Don Juan Manuel. Sánchez then proposed to the English ambassador that the king of England could send
these 150,000 ducats to Adorno. The ambassador liked the idea and said he would write about it in two ways. One was to
say that the money should be sent to Adorno. The other was to propose [new ...] to his king. In England, though, this
might be taken badly, because some there wish Adorno ill. If His Majesty approves, Sánchez thinks it should be said on
His Majesty's behalf that the money be sent to Adorno. The ambassador agreed and will write so.

**f. 141r.** [The Baron of ...] is here as French ambassador. Another French envoy came four days ago from Ferrara, about
the [absolution] and sentence given by [the Pope?]. The Signoria has letters from France, but they say nothing of
[moment], except that His Majesty's men and those of [`hod`] have [risen?]. News of the Turk: still good. Rhodes is in
danger ("pero el turco ha hovido muy gran danyo", in clear). Three days ago [...] of the plague. The clear date is
"en venecia a xxij de otubre de dxxij".

## Unknown code groups (absent from key_codes.tsv; proposals only, not applied)
- `hod` ×5. Contexts: "el hod que havia de ser el medio" (139r.09); "mal en el hod su embaxador" (139r.20); "el
  serenissimo? hod" (140r.01); "los de vuestra magestad y del hod" (141r.11). Suggests **Papa** (the Pope as mediator).
  In 139r.20 it may be a verb.
- `him` ×4. Contexts: "escrito a him al mif sobre el aposentarse la gente" (139v.17); "pues de him sera vuestra
  [magestad] havisada" (141r.07); "him de la pestilencia" (141r.22). No clear value.
- `mif` ×3. Contexts: "al mif sobre el aposentarse la gente del exercito" (139v.18); "respuesta del mif" (139v.21);
  "que el mif responda" (139v.22). Suggests a person or body, perhaps **duque (de Milan?)** or **Colegio**.
- `xec` ×3, always "havia xec-do con" (140r.18, 140v.01, 140v.04, 140v.07). Suggests **comunica-** or **trata-**
  ("havia comunicado / tratado con").
- `seb` ×2+, in "nuevos? seb-s" and "antes menos seb-s" (140r.05, 140v.19, 140v.23). Suggests **socorros** or
  **subsidios**.
- `sod` ×2, "los del sod" (139r.06, 139r.20). Suggests **governo / Consejo**. C03 proposed sod = govierno; the
  proposal is not applied, because the measure failed.
- `lud` ×2, "del lud" and "con la lud" (141r.10, 141r.12). Suggests **presente**, as C03 proposed; not applied.
- Also: `pod` ×2 ("de pod"), `sob` ×2 ("de sob", "armada de sob": a place?), `ref` ×2 ("por esta via de ref", a
  place?), `luf` ×2, `mag` (in "nonbra-te mag-mente"), and singletons: nal, muc, led, nof, nag, huc, vug, nad, feb, sub,
  deg, ni, rob, pug, mad, pem, vel, lec, vod, yip, yel, yog, val.

Sánchez to Charles V: the French party at Venice holding back letters, the Signoria's answer to Sánchez and the English ambassador (no obligation left to France, complaints of the detention of its goods), its letter to its nuncio for a universal peace. Clear date a vij de noviembre. Uncalendared.
